# Exterior Binding and Cover

The spine of this volume is bound in a **light-colored, textured parchment** <term>parchment: a durable material made from animal skin, used here to reinforce the "hinge" or spine of the book</term>. The material's quality suggests it is <insert>possibly vellum</insert> <note>Vellum is a high-grade parchment made from calfskin, prized for its longevity and smooth finish.</note>

The main cover board is decorated with **hand-marbled paper** showcasing a traditional swirl pattern.

### Decorative Pattern
The marbling displays an intricate "stone" or "swirl" design <note>Marbling is created by floating pigments on a liquid bath, swirling them into patterns, and then laying the paper on top to "lift" the design.</note> utilizing the following colors:
*   **Forest green**
*   **Terracotta orange**
*   **Ochre tan**

->The pattern features complex, organic shapes characteristic of hand-crafted bookbinding from the early modern period.<-

<summary>This page illustrates the manuscript's physical exterior, featuring a vellum-style parchment spine and decorative hand-marbled paper in earth tones. It showcases the traditional craftsmanship used to preserve and decorate historical documents.</summary>

### 113. (ANDREÄ, Johann Valentin). **The Chemical Wedding: of Christian Rosenkreutz. In the year 1459.** <note>original: "Chymische Hochzeit : Christiani Rosencreutz. Anno 1459." This is the third of the original Rosicrucian manifestos.</note> Strasbourg, Heirs of Lazarus Zetzner, 1616, small octavo <term>octavo: a book size resulting from folding a printed sheet into eight leaves (16 pages)</term> of 143 pages, half-vellum <term>half-vellum: a binding where the spine is covered in calf-skin parchment (vellum) while the rest of the cover is paper or cloth</term>.

With the printer’s mark on the title page.
Gardner, Rosicrucian Bibliography, 31, 3. Wolfstieg 42276, 3. Kloss 2476, 3. <note>These are citations to standard 19th-century bibliographic catalogs of occult and Masonic literature.</note> The "Chemical Wedding of Christian Rosenkreutz" is one of the primary writings of the purported founder of the Order of the Rose Cross. "He had only composed this work to amuse himself by criticizing and ridiculing the alchemists and the theosophists <note>theosophists: in this 17th-century context, those seeking direct knowledge of the divine through mystical philosophy</note> of that era" (Figuier) <note>referring to Louis Figuier (1819–1894), a prolific French science writer who often took a skeptical view of alchemy</note>. **Very rare.** — Good copy.

<summary>This page contains a detailed bibliographic entry for a rare 1616 edition of "The Chemical Wedding of Christian Rosenkreutz," a foundational Rosicrucian text. The entry includes a skeptical commentary suggesting the work was originally intended as a satire of the mystical movements of its time.</summary>

# THE CHEMICAL
# WEDDING:
## Christian Rosencreutz.
### In the year 1459.

->*Secrets made public grow worthless; and once profaned, they lose their grace.*<- <note>original Latin: "Arcana publicata vilescunt; & gratiam prophanata amittunt." This is a common warning in hermetic texts, suggesting that spiritual truths lose their "potency" or divine favor when exposed to those who cannot appreciate them.</note>

->*Therefore: do not cast pearls before swine, nor strew roses before an ass.*<- <note>original Latin: "Ergo: ne Margaritas objice porcis, seu Asino substerne rosas." This combines a biblical proverb from Matthew 7:6 with a pun on the "Rose" in the name Rosencreutz, warning the reader that this wisdom is not for the ignorant.</note>

->[Printer's Device: A seated lion holding a shield depicting an anchor and a ring, a symbol of stability and eternity]<-

->**STRASBOURG,**<-
->Published by the heirs of Lazarus Zetzner.<- <note>Lazarus Zetzner was a famous publisher in Strasbourg known for printing large collections of alchemical and philosophical works.</note>
->**In the year 1616.**<-

<summary>This is the title page of the first edition of "The Chemical Wedding of Christian Rosencreutz," featuring symbolic Latin warnings against revealing mystical secrets to the unworthy. It identifies the famous Strasbourg publisher Lazarus Zetzner and establishes the book's dual timeline: a fictional setting in 1459 and its real-world publication in 1616.</summary>

*Secrets made public become cheap; and once profaned, they lose their grace.* <note>original Latin: "Arcana publicata vilescunt: & gratiam prophanata amittunt." This is a classic Hermetic warning, suggesting that spiritual truths are diminished when shared with the uninitiated.</note>

*Therefore: do not cast pearls before swine, nor strew roses before an ass.* <note>original Latin: "Ergo: ne Margaritas objice porcis, seu Asino substernere rosas." This references the biblical warning in Matthew 7:6, while adding a clever pun on the "Rose" in Rosencreutz's name.</note>

# The First Book.
## Day 1. <note>original: "Dies I."</note>

<margin>Meditation.</margin>
**O**n an evening before Easter Day, I sat at a table, and as was my habit, I was conversing sufficiently with my Creator in my humble prayer, and meditating upon many great mysteries (of which the Father of Light, His Majesty, <note>A biblical reference to God as the source of all enlightenment (James 1:17).</note> has allowed me to see not a few). Now, having wished to prepare in my heart an unleavened, unspotted cake <note>The "unleavened cake" (Küchlein) is a metaphor for a pure soul, referencing the bread used during the Passover and Easter season.</note> together with my dear Easter Lamb, there suddenly came such a terrible wind that I thought for certain the mountain, into which my little house was dug, would burst apart from the great force. But because such things, and similar works of the Devil (who has done me much harm), did not trouble me, I took courage and remained in my meditation; until, contrary to my habit, someone touched me on the back, by which I was so terrified that I hardly dared to look around; yet I composed myself as joyfully as human weakness can be in such matters. And as this thing plucked at my coat several times, I looked around;
<margin>The Herald <note>original: "Præconi"</note></margin>
and there was a beautiful, magnificent woman, whose dress was entirely blue and set with golden stars, like the hea[vens]—

<summary>Christian Rosencreutz begins his narrative on the eve of Easter, describing a spiritual meditation interrupted by a violent storm and the appearance of a celestial female herald.</summary>
<keywords>Christian Rosencreutz, Easter, Meditation, Father of Light, Herald, spiritual trial, allegory</keywords>

<page-num>4</page-num>
# The Chymical Wedding:

—vens, elegantly adorned. In her right hand she carried a completely golden trumpet <term>trumpet <note>original: "Posaun." This instrument often signifies a divine announcement or the call to judgment.</note></term>, upon which a name was engraved that I could read well, but which I have been forbidden to reveal for now. In her left hand she had a large bundle of letters in all sorts of languages, which (as I learned later) she had to carry to all lands. She also had wings, large and beautiful, covered all over with eyes <note>A reference to the many-eyed celestial beings described in the biblical books of Ezekiel and Revelation, symbolizing divine all-seeing knowledge.</note>, with which she could soar upward and fly faster than any eagle. I might have been able to notice even more about her, but because she stayed with me for so short a time, and I was still filled with terror and amazement, I must leave it at that.

For as soon as I turned around, she flipped through her letters back and forth and finally pulled out a small letter, which she placed upon the table with great reverence <note>original: "Reverentz."</note>, and without a single word, she departed from me. In soaring up, however, she blew so powerfully into her beautiful trumpet that the whole mountain echoed from it, and for nearly a quarter of an hour afterward, I could hardly hear my own words.

In such an unforeseen adventure, I, poor soul, knew neither how to advise nor help myself; I therefore fell upon my knees and prayed to my Creator that He would allow nothing to happen to me against my eternal salvation. I then went with fear and trembling to the little letter; it was now so heavy that even if it had been made of pure gold, it could hardly have been so heavy. 

<margin>The Letter <note>original: "Epistola."</note></margin>
As I now examined it diligently, I found a small seal, there—

# Of Christian Rosencreutz.

<margin>The Seal.</margin>
...sealed with it. Upon it was engraved a delicate cross with the inscription: **In this sign † you shall conquer** <note>original: "In hoc signo vinces." This famous Latin phrase is historically associated with the conversion of Emperor Constantine, suggesting the divine and legitimate nature of this summons.</note>. As soon as I found this sign, I was greatly comforted, as I was not unaware that such a <term>seal <note>original: "Sigill." In alchemy and mysticism, a seal represents both a physical closure and a spiritual signature or authority.</note></term> would not be acceptable to the Devil, much less used by him. I therefore carefully opened the little letter: inside, upon a blue field with golden letters, I found the following verses written.

->Today, today, today,<-
->Is the King’s <term>Wedding <note>original: "Hochzeit." This refers to the "Chymical Wedding," a symbolic union of opposites (like soul and spirit, or mercury and sulfur) central to alchemical transformation.</note></term>,<-
->If you were born for this,<-
->Chosen by God for joy,<-
->You may go upon the mountain,<-
->Whereon three temples stand,<-
->And there behold the story.<-
->Keep watch,<-
->Examine yourself;<-
->If you do not diligently bathe,<-
->The Wedding may harm you.<-
->He suffers harm who lingers here;<-
->Let him beware who is too light.<-

->At the bottom stood: **Bridegroom & Bride** <note>original: "Sponsus & Sponsa."</note>.<-

<margin>Concerning the Wedding.</margin>
When I had read this letter, I felt as though I might faint; my hair stood on end, and cold sweat ran down my entire body. For although I realized that this was the intended Wedding of which I had been told in a physical vision seven years prior—and for which I had now, for so long a time...

<summary>Christian Rosencreutz opens the mysterious letter, finding a poetic invitation to a Royal Wedding that warns of the need for spiritual purity. The discovery triggers a physical reaction of dread and the memory of a vision from seven years earlier.</summary>
<keywords>The Seal, In hoc signo vinces, Chymical Wedding, spiritual trial, invitation, Bridegroom and Bride, Christian Rosencreutz</keywords>

# Chymical Wedding:

...drops that had been made. As I thus remained in such **misery** with others for a good while, and each constantly scolded the other for being blind and a prisoner, we finally heard many trumpets blowing together, and the kettle-drums beating so skillfully along with them, that it nevertheless refreshed and gladdened us in our <margin>Illumination <note>original: "Illustratio."</note></margin> **suffering** <note>original: "Creütz" (Cross). In this context, it refers to their shared burden or spiritual ordeal.</note>. Under such a sound, the lid at the top of the **Tower** was lifted, and a little light was let in to us. Then one could truly see us scrambling over one another; for everything went into a tumble, and it happened that he who raised himself too high fell under the feet of others. In short, every man wanted to be the uppermost; nor did I hold myself back, but with my heavy <term>fetters <note>original: "Springern." These are heavy shackles that bound the legs, forcing the prisoner to move in "springs" or hops. In alchemical allegory, these represent the weight of material existence.</note></term>, I nonetheless slipped out from among the others and raised myself up onto a <margin>Stone of support <note>original: "Lapis præsidii."</note></margin> **stone** that I had caught hold of, although I was also attacked there several times by others, against whom I defended myself as well as I could with hands and feet; for we thought nothing else than that we would all be set free, which however turned out quite differently. For after the Lords, who looked down upon us from the opening of the Tower, had taken some pleasure <note>original: "erlustriert." The narrator suggests the observers were entertained or enlightened by observing the desperate struggle below.</note> in such wriggling and whining, an old <margin>Warden of the prison <note>original: "Magister carceris."</note></margin> **ice-grey man** commanded us to be still; and as he had barely achieved this, he began to speak (as I still remember it) in this manner:

-> If it did not lift itself up, <-
-> The poor human race, <-

# Christian Rosencreutz

<margin>See St. Bernard, Sermon 3, on the 7 fragments. <note>original: "Vide S. Bernhard serm. 3 de 7. fragmentis." This refers to a medieval sermon by St. Bernard of Clairvaux regarding the fragments of bread gathered after Christ fed the multitude, often used as an allegory for gathering spiritual truths.</note></margin>

> To whom much good was granted
> By a Mother’s hand so true,
> Yet since they would not follow,
> They remain in such sorrow
> And must be held captive.
> Yet my dear Mother will not
> Heed their stubborn ways;
> She lets her beautiful treasures
> Come too much into the light—
> Though this happens very rarely,
> So that they may have some value;
> Otherwise, it would be thought a mere fable.
> Therefore, in honor of the feast
> Which we celebrate today,
> That her grace may be increased,
> She will do a good work:
> The **rope** will now be lowered;
> Whoever hangs onto it,
> He himself shall be set free.

<margin>Mistress of the Rope. <note>original: "Magistra Restis."</note></margin>
As she had barely finished speaking these words, the old woman commanded her servants to lower the **rope** into the **Tower** seven times, <margin>Seven times.</margin> and to pull up whoever could hang onto it. Oh, would to God I could sufficiently describe what unrest then arose among us! For everyone wanted to catch the rope, and yet each only hindered the other. <margin>First drawing out: 4 people.</margin> After seven minutes, however, a signal was given with a small bell. Upon this, the servants drew up four people the first time. At that moment, I could not come anywhere near the rope, as I—as previously mentioned—stayed by my...

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Christian Rosencreutz’s dream in the "Chymical Wedding." After the rope is lowered into the dark pit of the Tower to rescue prisoners, the narrator describes the chaotic struggle of the first several attempts.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>The Chymical Wedding:</header>

...greatest misfortune, had placed myself upon a stone by the wall of the **Tower**, and for that reason I could not reach the **rope**, which descended in the middle. 

<margin>Second drawing out. <note>original: "Secunda."</note></margin>
The second time the **rope** was let down, many found their chains too heavy, while their hands were too soft <note>This likely refers to those who lacked the spiritual "grip" or strength of character to hold on under pressure.</note>; they could not hold onto the **rope**, but instead knocked down many others who might have held on. Indeed, many a man was even dragged down by another who could not reach the rope himself; thus, even in our great misery, we were still envious of one another. But I pitied most of all those whose weight was so heavy that they tore the hands right out of their bodies, and yet they still could not be pulled up. 

So it happened that during those five turns, very few were drawn up. For as soon as the signal was given, the servants were so quick with the pulling that the majority of people tumbled over one another. Especially on the fifth time, the **rope** was drawn up completely empty. Because of this, the majority of us—including myself—despaired of our **deliverance** <term>release/freedom <note>original: "erledigung."</note></term> and called upon **God**, asking that He might have mercy on us and, if possible, deliver us from this darkness; and He then heard some among us.

<margin>Sixth drawing out. <note>original: "Sexta."</note></margin>
For when the **rope** came for the sixth time, several people hung onto it firmly. And because the **rope** swung back and forth as it was being pulled up, it—perhaps by Divine will—swung toward me. I quickly caught hold of it, sat on top of all the others, and so, finally, against all hope...

<summary>Christian Rosencreutz describes the desperate and violent struggle as prisoners attempt to grab a rope lowered into their dungeon. After several failed and despairing attempts, he finally manages to secure a place on the rope during the sixth pull.</summary>

# Christian Rosencreutz

<margin>A wound sustained from the tower. <note>original: "Vulnus ex turre contractū."</note></margin> ...hoped to come out, which greatly rejoiced me. ## Chemical Wedding

<margin>Prayer. <note>original: "Precatio"</note></margin>
I gave thanks and prayed that He would continue to keep me in His fear <note>A traditional term for living with a respectful, holy awe of God.</note>, fill my heart daily with wisdom and understanding, and finally bring me graciously to the desired end, despite my lack of merit. Thereupon I prepared myself for the journey.

<margin>Preparation for the journey. <note>original: "Preparatio ad iter"</note></margin>
I put on my white linen robe and girded my loins with a blood-red ribbon tied crosswise over my shoulders. On my hat, I fastened four red roses, so that I might be more easily recognized among the crowd by these signs. For provisions, I took bread, salt, and water. These I have used—on the advice of a wise man—at certain times and not without benefit in such cases. But before I departed from my little hut, I fell on my knees in this attire and wedding garment and prayed to God.

<margin>A Vow. <note>original: "Votum"</note></margin>
That if this call were true, He would let it lead to a good end for me. I also vowed before God’s face that if anything should be revealed to me through His grace, I would use it neither for honor nor for status in the world, but rather for the advancement of His name and in service to my fellow humans. With this vow and in good hope, I departed joyfully from my cell <term>a small, simple room often used for spiritual study and reflection <note>original: "Zellen"</note></term>.

Through us, the Bridegroom offers you a choice of four paths, through all of which—provided you do not wander into trackless ways—you may reach his Royal Court <term>the symbolic destination of the spiritual journey, representing union with the Divine <note>original: "Regiam"</note></term>. The **first** <margin>1.</margin> is short but dangerous, and one which will lead you among various rocks from which you will hardly be able to extricate yourself. The **second** <margin>2.</margin> is longer; it leads you around, not away; it is level and easy, if you do not allow yourself to be led away to the right or the left by the help of the Magnet <term>likely a reference to a compass or a spiritual "inner" attraction to the truth <note>original: "Magnetis auxilio"</note></term>. The **third** <margin>3.</margin> is truly Royal, which will make the way pleasant for you through various delights and spectacles of our King; but so far, this has happened to scarcely one in a thousand. By the **fourth** <margin>4.</margin> path, no living man shall reach the Royal Court,

As soon as I had read this writing, all my joy vanished again, and I, who had previously sung so cheerfully, now began to weep bitterly; for I saw all three paths before me, and I knew that I was permitted, at that time, to choose one path for myself. <margin>The path to be chosen by the author <note>original: "Via autoris Eligenda."</note></margin> Yet I feared that if I took the stony and rocky path, I might fall miserably to my death; or if I took the long path, I might either lose my way on a detour or otherwise succumb during the long journey. Nor did I dare hope that among a thousand, I should be the one to choose the **Royal Path**. The fourth I also saw before me, but it was so surrounded by fire and smoke that I dared not approach it at all. <margin>Doubt <note>original: "Dubium."</note></margin> I considered this back and forth, whether I should turn back or take one of the paths for myself. I well considered my unworthiness, but I was always comforted...

Meanwhile, he drew out a <margin>Token bought with salt.</margin> token <note>original Latin: "Tessera empta sale." In ancient times, a "tessera" was a small tablet used as a ticket or token of hospitality.</note> and asked me if I could redeem it. I ran so quickly that I did not even say goodbye to the keeper, so great was my anxiety! And indeed, it was truly necessary, for I could not run fast enough; the Virgin was already right behind me, and as she passed, all the lights were extinguished. I would never have been able to find the way if she had not still provided a glimmer of light with her torch. Necessity still drives me to...

### The Chymical Wedding:

<margin>Barbers <note>original Latin: "Balneatores." In the 17th century, barbers often handled bathing and grooming for guests.</note></margin>

# Of Christian Rosencreutz.

Therefore everyone kept silent and waited to see what would come of it. During this music, there were all kinds of stringed instruments that one could imagine, and they were tuned together in such harmony that I forgot myself and sat so motionless that my neighbors wondered at me; and this lasted almost half an hour, during which none of us spoke a word. For as soon as someone wanted to open their mouth, they were unexpectedly given a blow, and yet they did not know from where it came.
<margin>Penalty for the inattentive <note>original Latin: "Mulcta non attendentium." This refers to the invisible discipline enforced upon the guests to ensure they respected the performance.</note></margin>
It seemed to me—since we were not permitted to see any of the musicians—that I wished I could at least see all the <term>instruments</term><note>original Latin: "Instrumenta." These are the physical tools of the music which, in an alchemical context, often represent the harmony of the spheres.</note> they used. After half an hour, the music suddenly stopped, and we could see or hear nothing further.

Soon after, a great clatter and sound of trombones, trumpets, and kettle-drums arose before the hall door, and everything was so masterly as if the Roman Emperor were about to enter. Therefore the door opened itself, and the sound of the trombones became so loud that we could hardly bear it. Meanwhile, in my opinion, many thousands of little lights came into the hall, all moving along by themselves in proper order, so that we were completely terrified;
<margin>Little torches for the bed <note>original Latin: "Faculæ ad lectum." This may refer to the ceremonial lighting used when escorting a high-ranking person to their chambers or a place of rest.</note></margin>

You know that in the invitation letter:
No person was called hither
Who had not from God all beautiful gifts
Received long ago,
And who was adorned with every necessity,
As is fitting for such a matter;
Although they <note>The King and Bride, or the hosts</note> cannot now believe
That anyone would be so daring
Who, under such a heavy **condition**,
Would dare to present themselves in this case,
If he had not already for a long time
Prepared himself for this wedding.
Therefore, they stand in good hope,
Expecting all good things from you all;
They rejoice that in such a difficult time,
So many people have been found.
Yet humans are still so daring
That they do not set aside their coarseness,
And force their way into places
To which they have not been called.
So that no scoundrel <note>original German: "Bub," implying a common rogue.</note> may pass himself off here,
Or a knave <note>original German: "Schalck."</note> run among the others,
They instead—soon and without concealment—
Wish to have a pure wedding.

Where everyone will easily perceive
What he has left behind at home <note>i.e., what virtues or preparations they lack.</note>.
If there is anyone among this crowd
Who does not dare to trust himself at all...

# Christian Rosencreutz
<page-num>33</page-num>

And since I did not wish to depart in peace (as she had hoped), I quickly reflected; <margin>The author humbles himself.</margin> and because my conscience convicted me of all my ignorance and unworthiness, I decided to remain in the hall with the others and be much more content with the meal I had already received than to await future blows with danger. After one guest here and another there had been led by their little lights into a chamber (each into his own, as I later learned), nine of us remained, and among them was the one who had spoken with me at the table earlier. Although our little lights did not leave us, soon after an hour one of the aforementioned pages came, bringing a large bundle of ropes with him. He first asked whether we were determined to stay; when we consented with sighs, he bound each of us in a separate place, <margin>A sorrowful night.</margin> and then departed with our little lights, leaving us poor souls in the dark. Then, for the first time, tears began to flow from many an eye <note>original: "vber die Körb zulauffen," literally "to run over the baskets," an idiomatic expression for heavy weeping.</note>, and I myself could not refrain from crying. For although we were not forbidden from speaking, our pain and sorrow allowed no one to say a word. Furthermore, the ropes were so strangely made that no one could cut them, much less pull them off their feet. Still, I was comforted by the thought that for many who had now gone to their rest, their supposed gain would soon turn to great shame; whereas we, in a single night, [would atone for] all our presumption—

A Symbolic Dream. <note>original: "Somnium Typicum."</note>
What would happen with the test of the scales.
He who climbs high, falls hard.

...lowliness <note>The text completes the word "Geringheit" from the previous page, meaning "smallness" or "humility."</note> could atone for our failings. Finally, I fell asleep amidst my heavy thoughts. For although only a few of us could close our eyes, I could not help myself because of the exhaustion. In such a sleep, I had a dream; and although there is not much to it, I do not consider it unnecessary to recount: It seemed to me as if I were upon a high mountain, and saw before me a great and wide valley. In this valley was gathered an unspeakable multitude of people, each of whom had a thread upon his head, by which he was suspended from heaven. Now, one hung high, another low, and some even stood upon the earth. An old man flew about in the air, holding a pair of scissors in his hand, with which he cut the thread of one here and another there. He who was already near the earth was finished all the sooner and fell without a sound. But when he came to one who hung high, that person fell so hard that the earth trembled. It happened for some that their thread was lengthened, so that they reached the earth before the thread was cut. I took delight in such tumbling, and rejoiced from my heart whenever one—who had long boasted in the air of his wedding—fell down so shamefully and perhaps even took several of his neighbors with him. Likewise, I was glad when one who had kept himself near the earth at all times could depart so quietly and peacefully that even <margin>Waking up. <note>original: "Expergef," an abbreviation for the Latin "Expergefactus."</note></margin> his neighbors did not notice it. But just as I...

### The Third Day <note>original: "III. Dies"</note>

<margin>
The conversation of those rising.

As soon as the dear day had dawned, and the bright sun had risen above the mountains and was once again set to its commanded duty in the high heavens, my good "warriors" <note>The narrator uses "warriors" (Kämpffer) somewhat ironically to describe the ambitious guests who had sought honors at the wedding.</note> began to rise from their beds and gradually prepare themselves for the inquiry <note>The "inquisition" or examination that the guests must undergo to prove their worthiness.</note>. Yet, they simultaneously argued that what had happened here should not be held against them in the worst light. For, since once 
<margin>An excuse. <note>original: "Excusatio."</note></margin>
the Lords wished to enter the castle and had also promised great sums of money for it, everyone had used every trick to snatch something, and had thus brought matters as far as was now visible before everyone's eyes. But that it did not succeed, they believed they had spoiled no more than the Lords themselves: who should have possessed enough understanding to know that if someone could have entered here safely, he would not—for the sake of a meager profit—have climbed over the walls with them at such great peril. Furthermore, their books had been bought up so frequently that anyone who could not otherwise support himself was forced to begin such a deception. They also hoped that if one judged fairly, no wrongdoing would be found in them, as they had only behaved toward the Lords as servants should, acting upon their diligent request. With such and similar words, they sought to excuse themselves. But it was 
<margin>A refutation. <note>original: "Refutatio."</note></margin>

<margin>A draught of oblivion <note>original: "Haustus oblivionis."</note></margin>
Each was given a **draught of oblivion** from a goblet, so that they might thus forget their misfortune.

After this, the volunteers <note>Those who admitted their lack of worthiness before the weighing began.</note> departed; because of their honesty, they were allowed to **pass through**, though they were never to return in such a state. However, should more be revealed to them in the future, just as to the others, they would be welcome guests.

<margin>The condemned <note>original: "Damnati."</note></margin>
Meanwhile, the removal of the others was underway, in which once again a distinction was made according to each person's transgression. Some were sent away naked and unharmed; some were driven out with little bells and tinkling bells. Some were whipped out. In short, the punishments were so varied that I cannot recount them all. Finally, it came to the very last ones, whose business took somewhat longer: for a good amount of time passed until some were hanged, some beheaded, some plunged into the water, and others dispatched in different ways.

<margin>Expression of compassion <note>original: "Commiserationis expositio."</note></margin>
At such an **execution**, my eyes truly overflowed; not indeed because of the punishment, which they had otherwise well deserved for their arrogance, but in consideration of human blindness—that we are constantly toiling at that which has been sealed to us ever since the first Fall <note>original: "ersten fall," a reference to the biblical Fall of Man, suggesting that human effort alone cannot regain what was lost in Eden.</note>. Thus the garden, which shortly before had been completely full, was soon emptied, so that except for the soldiers, not a soul remained.

<margin>Music.</margin>

<margin>Departure from the Laboratory.</margin>
We were satisfied for this time and departed with our virgins. Our musicians were already waiting for us and led us back down the <term>Spiral Staircase</term> <note>original: "Schnecken," literally "snail," referring to a winding or screw-like staircase.</note>; however, the door was carefully closed and bolted behind us.

<margin>The virgins joke about the author's old age.</margin>
When we had returned to the hall, one of the virgins began: "Sister, I wonder that you dared to venture among so many people." "My sister," replied our <term>President</term> <note>original: "Presidentin," the woman leading the group of virgins.</note>, "I feared no one so much as him," pointing at me. These words went straight to my heart, for I understood well that she was mocking my age. And indeed, I was the oldest of all of them. Yet she comforted me again with the promise that if I behaved myself well with her, she would happily help rid me of this burden <note>The "burden" of his old age.</note>.

<margin>Banquet with the virgins.</margin>
Meanwhile, the meal was served again, and a virgin was seated beside each of us. They knew well how to pass the time with gracious conversation.

# Christian Rosencreutz. 79

<margin>Table talk.</margin>
But as for what their conversation and entertainment were, I must not **blab out of school** <note>original: "auß der Schul schwäzen," an idiom meaning to reveal secrets or private matters discussed in confidence.</note>. Most of the questions, however, concerned the **arts**, from which I could easily perceive that young and old alike were occupied with learning. Yet it was always on my mind how I might become young again.

<margin>The author is sorrowful because of old age.</margin>
For that reason, I was somewhat sorrowful. The Virgin noticed this and therefore began: "I notice well what this young fellow lacks. What would it be worth if I slept with him tonight? He would be more cheerful tomorrow."

At this they began to laugh, and although I **blushed** all over, I yet had to laugh at my own misfortune. 

<margin>He receives playful comfort from the virgin.</margin>
Now there was one there who wanted to avenge my shame upon the Virgin. 

<margin>A companion.</margin>
He spoke thus: "I hope that not only we, but also the virgins themselves, will bear witness for our brother that our Lady President promised to sleep with him tonight."

"I would be quite content with that," answered the Virgin, "if I did not have to fear these sisters of mine; it would not do for them if 

<margin>The Virgin Lucifera.</margin>
I, without their consent, chose the handsomest and best for myself." 

"My sister," another soon began, "we perceive here that your high office has not made you proud. <margin>The principal things that took place <note>original: "Præcipua quæ agebantur."</note></margin>
Since there is much of significance to consider in this comedy, I did not want to omit describing it briefly.

<margin>Act 1 <note>original: "Actus. 1."</note></margin>

Wherefore the King soon opened it, and having read it, he wept over it; thereupon he pointed out to his servants with what great harm the **Moorish King** <note>A common literary trope in early modern European drama, the "Moorish King" often symbolized a foreign, usurping, or "infidel" antagonist.</note> had seized the land of his cousin and had exterminated all the royal offspring except for this child. He therefore deliberated how he might encounter such a powerful enemy with trickery, which finally succeeded for him through some who had fled to him out of famine. And, against everyone's expectations, the young maiden fell back into his hands; he would then have had her strangled immediately, had he not been wonderfully deceived by his own servants. Thus, this **Act** was also concluded with a wonderful triumph for the Moor.

Upon this, in the third **scene** of this **act**, he had her led out: first stripped completely naked, bound to a column on a rough wooden scaffold, and soundly scourged. Finally, she was also condemned to death. This was so pitiful to behold that it brought tears to many eyes. Thus she was thrown naked into the dungeon <note>original: "Kercker"</note> to await death, which was to be administered by poison; however, it did not kill her but made her entirely <term>leprous</term> <note>original: "außsetzig"; leprosy here symbolizes a state of extreme spiritual or physical corruption and the need for purification.</note>. ...to such a quiet manner that I am afraid to say much about it. Here I cannot leave unmentioned how all the Royal persons, before eating, dressed themselves in snow-white, shining garments and thus sat at the table. <margin>The crown above the table <note>original: "Corona super mensam"</note></margin> Above the table hung the previously mentioned large golden crown, whose precious stones might well have illuminated the hall without any other light.

However, I did notice that the young King sometimes sent food to the white snake on the little altar, which also gave me much to think about. <margin>Cupid was the merriest <note>original: "Cupido war der lustigste"</note></margin> The chatter of this banquet <term>banquet<note>The original "Pancket" refers to a formal feast or celebratory meal.</note></term> came almost entirely from little Cupid, who could not leave us—and me especially—undisturbed. He was forever bringing something strange up for discussion. But there was no special joy; everything happened quietly. From this, I could imagine some great future danger for myself, especially since no music was heard. Instead, if we were asked anything, <margin>Short speeches <note>original: "Sermones breves"</note></margin> we had to give short, direct answers and leave it at that. In short, everything had such a strange appearance that sweat began to run over my body, and I well believe that even the bravest man’s courage could have failed him. <margin>Speech of the Young King <note>original: "Oratio Regis Adolescentis"</note></margin> Now, when this supper had almost come to an end, the young King commanded the book to be handed to him from the little altar; this he did—

As soon as they reached the shore, I soon saw our **Virgin** with a torch approaching the ships. As soon as the coffins were placed in the ships, all the lights were extinguished. Then the six flames sailed together across the lake, so that no more than a single small light remained in each ship as a watch. Several hundred guards had also camped along the shore, and the Virgin was sent back into the castle, which she carefully bolted shut again. From this, I could well judge that nothing more would happen tonight, and we had to wait for the day; so we returned to rest.

<margin>The author alone saw these things. <note>original Latin: "Auctor solus hæc vidit."</note></margin>

<margin>The Tower of Olympus.</margin> <note>original: "Turris Olympi"; in alchemical allegory, this tower represents the Athanor, the specialized furnace where the final stages of the "Great Work" take place.</note>
The Tower of Olympus came into sight; for this reason the Virgin commanded that a signal of our arrival be given with several shots <note>likely cannon or mortar fire</note>, which was then done. Immediately we saw a large white flag being hung out, and a small gilded boat coming out to meet us. When this reached us, it was an old man, the <margin>The Warden.</margin> Warden of the Tower <note>original: "Custos"</note>, with several attendants clothed in white, by whom we were kindly received and así led to the Tower. <margin>The Structure.</margin> This Tower stood upon a perfectly square island, which was surrounded by a rampart <note>original: "Wahl"; a defensive wall or bulwark.</note> so firm and thick that I myself counted 260 steps through its thickness. <margin>Day.</margin> <note>Referring to the Fourth Day of the journey.</note> Beyond the rampart was a fine meadow with several small gardens, in which grew strange and unknown fruits; and then there was yet another wall around the Tower. The Tower itself was as if seven round towers had been built together, yet the middle one was somewhat higher, and they all connected internally, having seven stories one upon another.

<margin>1st Chamber.</margin> <note>original: "1. Conclave"</note>
When we had thus come to the doors of the Tower, we were led a little aside upon the walls so that—as I well noticed—the coffins <note>original: "Sarch"; containing the bodies of the kings and queens beheaded earlier in the narrative.</note> could be brought into the Tower without our knowledge; the others knew nothing of this. As soon as this was done, we were led into the lowest part of the Tower, which was nonetheless beautifully painted. But we had little leisure here, for this was nothing other than a laboratory. <margin>The guests' labors.</margin> <note>original: "Labores hospitium"</note> There we were required to work with herbs, precious stones, and all sorts of...

So, for a good while I looked out over the sea, and just as it was midnight, as soon as it struck twelve o'clock, I saw from afar seven flames <note>The seven flames often represent the seven planetary influences or the seven metals in alchemy, signaling a spiritual descent or transformation.</note> traveling over the sea, and they moved directly toward the top of the tower. This brought me some fear, for as soon as the flames settled, the winds began to make the sea very stormy. The moon was also covered by clouds, and my joy was ended with such fear that I hardly had enough time to find the steps again and return into the tower. Whether the flames stayed longer or flew away again, I cannot say, for in such darkness I did not dare venture out again. So I lay down upon my quilt <note>original: "Kolter"; a coarse cloth or heavy blanket.</note>, and since the fountain in our laboratory <note>original: "Laboratorio"</note> murmured so sweetly and quietly, I fell asleep all the sooner. And so this fifth day was also concluded with wonders.

Some thought they had not been killed at all, but others had been decapitated in their place. As we were now quite...

<margin>The guests' skill in fire-working is praised.</margin> <note>original: "Pyrotehnia hospitum laudatur." <term>Pyrotechnia</term> in this context refers to the alchemical art of regulating fire and heat to achieve specific chemical transformations.</note>
At this, we behaved ourselves in such a way that he was obliged to let our diligence pass. We said we wished to choose. "No," answered the old man, "it must be by lot."
<margin>The Lot.</margin> <note>original: "Sors."</note>
With this, he made three little notes; on one he wrote "Ladder", on the second "Rope", on the third "Wings". These he placed in a hat, and each had to draw; what he received, that remained his. Those who received the ropes thought they had the best of it; but a ladder fell to me, which distressed me greatly, for it was twelve feet <note>original: "Schuch", literally "shoes," a traditional unit of measurement.</note> long and quite heavy, which I had to take upon myself. The others could wind their ropes flexibly around themselves, while for the third group, the old man attached the wings so skillfully as if they had grown there naturally. With this, he drew a tap <note>original: "Hanen", literally a "cock" or faucet.</note>, so that the fountain no longer ran, and we had to clear it away from the center. After everything had been carried out, he took the little chest with the glasses with him, took his leave, and locked the door behind him.

...could come up; even though we had to exert ourselves so much, I could well judge that there was something special about this, and we had to let the old man <note>The warden or governor of the Tower who oversees the alchemical process.</note> have something to do as well. For even their wings were of no use to those others: for when they wanted to come up through the hole, <note>This refers to the companions who were previously given wings, yet still struggled with the physical ascent of the tower.</note>

once we had also overcome this, and the hole had been closed, I saw the globe hanging in the middle of the hall by a strong chain. In this hall, there was nothing but windows, and between every two windows was a door. These doors covered nothing other than a large, polished mirror. And these windows and mirrors were **optically** <note>original: "opticè"; refers to the mathematical and physical study of light and reflection.</note> directed toward one another, so that although the sun (which at that time shone beyond measure brightly) only struck the door.

<margin>Artistic works <note>original: "Artif. opera"</note></margin>
Yet (after the windows were opened toward the sun, and the doors in front of the mirrors were drawn open), there was nothing but sun in the entire hall and in all places; through artificial <term>refraction</term> <note>The bending and focusing of light rays, here used to concentrate solar heat onto the alchemical vessel.</note> the rays all struck the golden globe which hung in the middle. And because the globe was already highly polished, it gave off such a brilliance that none of us could keep our eyes open.

<margin>Miraculous spectacle <note>original: "Mirac. spect"</note></margin>
For that reason, we had to look out of the windows until the globe was well heated and brought to the desired effect. Here I may well say that I saw in these mirrors the most wonderful sight that Nature has ever brought to light; for there were suns in all places, and the globe in the middle shone even brighter, so that we [could see] it as well as the sun itself...

...could not endure it for a single moment. It seemed good to us then, because we were able to have a <term>break</term> <note>original: "Vacantz"; a temporary cessation of labor.</note>, to refresh ourselves a little with breakfast.

<margin>Philosophical Breakfast <note>original: "Prandium Philosoph."</note></margin>
This meal was once again truly philosophical, and we had no need to fear any compulsion toward excess, yet we lacked for nothing. Thus, the hope of future joy (of which the Virgin constantly reassured us) made us so merry that we paid no heed to any labor or inconvenience. Likewise, I can truthfully say of my companions of such high rank, that they never turned their thoughts toward their kitchen or table, but their pleasure was solely to be present for such <term>marvelous physics</term> <note>original: "Abentheurlichen Physic"; in this context, "physics" refers to natural philosophy and the study of the hidden laws of nature through alchemy.</note>, and to contemplate therein the Creator's wisdom and omnipotence. After taking our snack, we prepared ourselves for work again, for the globe was sufficiently cooled. We had to lift it from the chain onto the floor with great effort and labor.

<margin>Opening of the Globe <note>original: "Resolutio Globi."</note></margin>
Now there was a <term>discussion</term> <note>original: "disputation"; a formal debate among the scholars on the best technical approach.</note> as to how we might get the globe apart, for we were commanded to cut it exactly in half. Finally, a pointed diamond had to do the best service. When we had thus opened the globe, nothing raw remained, but rather a beautiful, large, **snow-white egg**: This gladdened us to the highest degree, for it had turned out so well. For the Virgin...

...was worried that the shell might perhaps still be too soft. We stood around this egg with joy, as if we had laid it ourselves.

<margin>4th Chamber <note>original: "4. conclave."</note></margin>
In this hall, we found a large copper kettle filled with yellow sand, which was being warmed by a modest little fire. The egg was then buried within it so that it might fully **mature** <note>This stage mimics the natural incubation of a bird's egg, but here it represents the "digestion" phase of alchemy, where steady, gentle heat perfects the substance.</note> there. This kettle was square; on one side, these two verses were written in large letters:

->**O, BLI. TO. BIT. MI. LI.**<-
->**KANT. I. VOLT. BIT. TO. GOLT.**<-
<note>These cryptic syllables are a phonetic puzzle in the original German. When read aloud, they sound like: "O Blei, du bist mir lieb; Kein Gift wollt' ich, bist du Gold" — which translates to: "O lead, you are dear to me; No poison I would want, if you were gold." It highlights the alchemist's goal of turning lead into gold.</note>

On the other side were these three words:

->**HEALTH. SNOW. SPEAR.**<-
<note>original: "SANITAS. NIX. HASTA." In alchemical lore, "Health" refers to the "medicine" of the Stone; "Snow" to the white stage of the work (albedo); and the "Spear" can represent the penetrating power of the spirit or a specific chemical action.</note>

The third side had nothing more than this single word:

->**L. E. T. I. T. B. E. D. O. N. E.**<-
<note>original: "F. I: A. T." This is the Latin "Fiat," the same word used in Genesis ("Let there be light"), suggesting the alchemical work is a reflection of divine creation.</note>

But on the back stood a complete **inscription** <note>original: "Inscription."</note>, reading as follows:

<margin>He is bound. He is fed with the blood of the beheaded. He is fed with the blood of another King. He becomes iridescent. <note>original: "Vincitur. Pascitur sanguine decollatorum. Sanguine alius Regis pascitur. Iridescit."</note></margin>
...he would give us all enough to do. This indeed soon came to pass. He bit and scratched so fiercely around him that if he could have had anyone at his mercy, he would soon have been finished with him. Now he was entirely black and wild <note>This represents the *Nigredo* or "blackness" stage, associated with chaos and the raw state of matter.</note>; therefore, other food was brought to him: perhaps the blood of another royal person. From this, all his black feathers fell out again, and in their place grew other snow-white feathers <note>This represents the *Albedo* or "whiteness" stage, signifying purification.</note>. Thus he was also somewhat tamer and allowed himself to be handled more easily, yet we still did not trust him. From the third feeding, his feathers began to become so beautifully colored that in all my life I had never seen colors so beautiful <note>This is the *Cauda Pavonis* or "Peacock's Tail" stage, where many colors appear before the final goal is reached.</note>. He was also beyond measure tame and behaved so friendly toward us that, with the Virgin's permission, we released him from his captivity.

<margin>He is freed from bonds. <note>original: "Liberatur vinculis."</note></margin>

With this, she commanded that the midday meal be served so that we might recover ourselves, since the most troublesome work was now over, and it was also proper that we begin to enjoy the fruits of our labor—

the **Maiden** began to speak to us in this way: "Dear sirs, we are here in the sixth hall, and have no more than one still before us, wherewith our labor ends, and we shall travel home again to our Castle to awaken our most Gracious Lords and Ladies. Now, I could nonetheless wish that you all together, as you are here with one another, had behaved yourselves in such a way that I could report your praise to our highly esteemed King and Queen and might have obtained a worthy reward. <margin>The true labor under the roof <note>original: "Verus labor sub tecto"</note></margin>
Our work was this: we had to moisten the ashes with our previously prepared water, so that they became just like a thin dough. And this blowing lasted so long until our old man urged us back to work, so that I cannot say what happened afterward.) 

We opened the molds, and there were two beautiful, bright, and almost transparent little figures, the likes of which human eyes had never seen: a little boy and a little girl.

These angel-beautiful little children we first laid upon two little satin cushions, and gazed at them for a good while, so that we almost [forgot ourselves] <note>The text ends mid-sentence; "vergē" is likely the start of "vergessen," meaning to forget or lose oneself in the sight.</note> over such a magnificent spectacle.

...[humanity] they became. The old man held us back and commanded us to let fall, one drop after another, the Bird's blood (which had been caught in the little golden bowl) into the mouths of the little figures. From this they visibly increased in size, and whereas they had been small before, they were now even more beautiful in their proportions; truly all painters should have been here and felt ashamed of their art compared to this creation of nature.

Now they began to grow so large that we had to lift them from the little cushion and lay them upon a long table which had been covered with white velvet. The old man also commanded us to cover them with a white, delicate double-taffeta <note>A high-quality, thick silk fabric.</note> up to their chests, 

<margin>Most beautiful ones. <note>original: "Pulcherrimi."</note></margin>
which was almost against our will because of their unspeakable beauty. But to be brief, before we had even used up all the blood, they were already at full adult size and had golden-yellow curly hair. And the aforementioned image of Venus <note>Christian Rosencreutz previously stumbled upon a secret chamber containing the physical, sleeping form of Venus; he notes here that these new creations surpass even her beauty.</note> was nothing compared to them. 

But as yet there was no natural warmth or sensitivity, but only dead images, though of a lively and natural color. And because it was feared they might grow too large, the old man would not let them be given anything more, but covered their faces completely with the cloth, and had torches placed all around the table. <margin>Forms and resurrection. <note>original: "Formæ et resurre." likely "Formae et resurrectio."</note></margin>

# The Chymical Wedding:

...but also to swear to the following <term>articles <note>original: "Articul"; the specific rules or bylaws of the knightly order.</note></term>. Then His Royal Majesty will know once again how to conduct himself toward his allies. Thereupon, he had the boy read the articles aloud; they were these:

I. You, Lord Knights, shall swear that you will at all times attribute your Order to no devil or spirit, but to God alone, your Creator, and to His handmaid, Nature.

II. That you will hold in hatred all fornication, unchastity, and impurity, and not defile your Order with such vices.

III. That you will use your gifts to come to the aid of everyone who is worthy and in need of them.

IV. That you do not desire to use such honor for worldly pomp and high standing.

V. That you do not wish to live longer than God wills.

<margin>Privileges.</margin>
We had to laugh quite a bit at this last article; it may well have been added only as a jest <note>The guests likely found the idea of swearing not to seek immortality amusing, as the quest for the "Elixir of Life" was a common goal in alchemy.</note>. Be that as it may, we had to swear by the King’s scepter. Thereupon, we were installed as knights with the customary <term>solemnity <note>original: "Solennitet"; refers to the formal, ritualistic pomp of the inauguration.</note></term> and, among other privileges, were placed in authority over **Ignorance, Poverty, and Sickness.**

<summary>Christian Rosencreutz and his companions swear five vows to join the knightly order, emphasizing devotion to God and Nature, charity, and humility. Following the ceremony, they are granted symbolic power over the human afflictions of ignorance, poverty, and illness.</summary>

<margin>The author confessing his guilt. <note>original Latin: "Autor reus confitens."</note></margin>

The King and the other lords marveled greatly at such an unexpected confession: They thus commanded me to step aside for a while. As soon as I was called forward again, **Atlas** <note>The astronomer and teacher who guides the guests through the castle's mysteries.</note> informed me: It was indeed painful for His Royal Majesty that I, whom they loved above others, had fallen into such a mishap; but because it was not possible for them to overstep their **ancient tradition** <note>original: "herkommen," referring to the immutable laws or customs of the castle.</note>, they knew no other way to absolve me than that the other man should be set free, and I should take his place; they hoped that another would soon commit a transgression, so that I might thus return home again. 

<margin>He hears the sentence. <note>original Latin: "Audit sententiam."</note></margin>

At the same time, no release could be hoped for before the wedding feast of their future son. This **judgment** nearly cost me my life, and I was at first an enemy to myself and my **talkative mouth** <note>original: "Verschwaßten Maul." Christian regrets that his own honesty and inability to keep a secret led to his downfall.</note>, that I had not been able to keep silent; yet I finally took heart, and because I thought it must be so, I reported how this guardian had gifted me with a sign and recommended me to the others. 

<margin>Praise of the porter’s kindness. <note>original Latin: "Laus beneficii portitoris."</note></margin>

# Front Cover of the Volume

The volume is bound in a striking example of historical craftsmanship. The exterior features **marbled paper** <term>a decorative paper produced by floating pigments on a liquid bath and transferring the pattern to the sheet</term> with a dynamic swirl pattern. 

->**Decorative Details**<-

The color palette consists of deep reds, vibrant oranges, and soft blue-green hues. These patterns were not only aesthetic but also served to hide wear and handling marks on frequently used books. The spine and edges of the volume appear to be reinforced with **vellum** <term>high-quality parchment prepared from animal skin, known for its extreme durability</term>, a common practice in seventeenth-century bookbinding to ensure the longevity of the manuscript.

The cover shows minimal wear, preserving the vividness of the pigments and the integrity of the binding materials. This specific swirl pattern is indicative of artisanal techniques used in European scriptoria and binderies during the early modern period.

<summary>This page represents the decorative front cover of the manuscript, featuring a well-preserved swirl-patterned marbled paper typical of historical bookbinding.</summary>
<keywords>marbled paper, bookbinding, vellum, front cover, swirl pattern, seventeenth-century</keywords>
